1986 · Question 23

Mammalian blood circulation is called double circulation because blood

  1. Passes through the heart twice in a complete circuit
  2. Moves in both arteries and veins
  3. Circulates in both the heart and other organs
  4. Enters a heart containing atria and ventricles
Answer and explanation

A: Passes through the heart twice in a complete circuit

Blood travels from the heart to the lungs and back, then from the heart to the body tissues and back. These pulmonary and systemic circuits make two passages through the heart in one complete journey through both circuits.

1986 · Question 24

During stomatal transpiration, which is the correct outward sequence for water? 1. Mesophyll; 2. Leaf veins; 3. Substomatal cavity; 4. Stomata.

  1. 3 → 2 → 1 → 4
  2. 2 → 3 → 1 → 4
  3. 2 → 1 → 3 → 4
  4. 1 → 2 → 3 → 4
Answer and explanation

C: 2 → 1 → 3 → 4

Water arrives in the leaf veins, moves into mesophyll tissues and evaporates into internal air spaces. Water vapour then diffuses through substomatal spaces and out through stomata. Thus the sequence is 2 → 1 → 3 → 4.

1986 · Question 25

  1. Auricular valve
  2. Tricuspid valve
  3. Ventricular valve
  4. Bicuspid valve
Answer and explanation

D: Bicuspid valve

The bicuspid, or mitral, valve lies between the left atrium and left ventricle. It closes during ventricular contraction to prevent blood flowing back into the atrium. The tricuspid valve occupies the corresponding opening on the right side.

1986 · Question 27

Which statement is NOT correct during normal quiet inhalation in mammals?

  1. External intercostal muscles contract
  2. The diaphragm rises into a more domed position
  3. The ribs rise
  4. Pressure inside the lungs falls below atmospheric pressure
Answer and explanation

B: The diaphragm rises into a more domed position

During inhalation, the diaphragm contracts and flattens downwards, while the external intercostal muscles help raise the ribs. The expanding chest allows lung volume to increase and pressure to fall, drawing air in. A rising, more domed diaphragm is associated with relaxation during expiration.

1986 · Question 28

Which equation summarises alcoholic fermentation of glucose?

  1. C6H12O6 + 2C2H5OH + 2CO2
  2. 6CO2 + 6H2O → C6H12O6 + 6O2
  3. C6H12O6 + 6O2 → 6CO2 + 6H2O + energy
  4. C6H12O6 → 2C2H5OH + 2CO2 + energy
Answer and explanation

D: C6H12O6 → 2C2H5OH + 2CO2 + energy

Alcoholic fermentation converts glucose into ethanol and carbon dioxide without consuming oxygen in the net equation. Coupled glycolysis captures a small amount of usable energy. The balanced products contain the same total numbers of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen atoms as the glucose.

  1. Sebaceous gland

  4. Malpighian layer

The living epidermal layers traditionally called the Malpighian layer include keratin-producing cells and cells associated with melanin production or storage. Melanin contributes pigmentation, while keratin helps give the epidermis mechanical strength.

1986 · Question 32

  4. Transverse foramina and a large body
Answer and explanation

A: A short spinous process and transverse foramina

Typical mid-cervical vertebrae have transverse foramina and relatively small bodies, with short spinous processes. The atlas, axis and seventh cervical vertebra have special features, so the typical pattern should not be applied unchanged to every neck vertebra.

1986 · Question 33

A nastic plant movement is characterised by a direction that is

  1. Determined only by the direction of light
  2. Independent of the direction from which the stimulus comes
  3. Directed towards or away from the stimulus
  4. Determined only by an internal stimulus
Answer and explanation

B: Independent of the direction from which the stimulus comes

Nastic movements have a direction determined by the plant’s structure rather than the direction of the triggering stimulus. A leaf may fold after touch regardless of which side was touched. A directional growth response towards or away from a stimulus is a tropism.

AI-reviewed · source checked

Enable JavaScript to verify this question's diagram before viewing its answer.

In the maize-grain section shown, which labelled part develops into the new plant?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Answer and explanation

B: 3

Part 3 marks the embryo region, which contains the developing shoot and root. During germination these grow into the seedling. Stored food supports this growth, but the food store and the grain’s attachment do not themselves become the new plant.

Answer and explanation

Label 1 points to the protective outer covering. A maize grain is a caryopsis, in which the seed coat is closely fused with the fruit wall. The plumule and radicle are developing shoot and root structures located within the embryo.

  4. Maintain the shape of the grain
Answer and explanation

Part 2 is the endosperm, which stores nutrients, particularly starch, that support the embryo during germination. These reserves are mobilised while the young seedling is becoming able to obtain resources through its roots and leaves.

1986 · Question 37

Gestation in a mammal is the period

  1. Of growth after birth
  2. Beginning only when the fetal stage starts and ending at birth
  3. Of development from fertilisation to birth
  4. Before the zygote forms
Answer and explanation

C: Of development from fertilisation to birth

Gestation encompasses prenatal development from fertilisation until birth, including the early embryonic stages and later fetal development. It does not begin only when the embryo is called a fetus and does not include postnatal growth.

  3. Body weight
  4. Height
Answer and explanation

B: Preferred handedness, such as left-handed or right-handed

Hand preference can be recorded as categories, including left, right or mixed preference. Height, body weight and pigmentation intensity can vary across a numerical range. Categorical recording does not imply that handedness is controlled by a single gene.

1986 · Question 41

Which instrument rotates a plant to help investigate responses to directional light or gravity?

  1. Auxanometer
  2. Potometer
  3. Clinostat
  4. Photometer
Answer and explanation

C: Clinostat

A clinostat turns the plant so that a stimulus does not act persistently from one direction relative to it. Comparing suitable rotating and stationary setups helps investigate tropic responses. Rotation does not remove gravity; it changes the plant’s orientation relative to it.

1986 · Question 42

At a trophic level where animals feed directly on plants, they act as

  1. Secondary consumers
  2. Primary consumers
  3. Producers
  4. Predators of animals
Answer and explanation

B: Primary consumers

Plants are producers because they make organic material using an external energy source. An animal eating those plants obtains food directly from the producer level and acts as a primary consumer. Eating a primary consumer would place an animal at a higher trophic level.

1986 · Question 43

Which list contains only biotic components of an agricultural ecosystem?

  1. Crops, pests and beneficial insects
  2. Crops, temperature and humidity
  3. Pests, beneficial insects and water
  4. Crops, water and soil
Answer and explanation

A: Crops, pests and beneficial insects

Biotic components are living organisms, such as crop plants and insects. Temperature, humidity and water are abiotic conditions or materials. Soil contains living organisms but soil as a whole is not simply a living organism.

  3. Cholera

Answer and explanation

There is no available vaccine for preventing onchocerciasis. Vaccines exist for polio, cholera and tuberculosis, although their protection and recommended uses differ. BCG is particularly important against severe childhood forms of tuberculosis.

  2. Decomposers
  3. Scavengers
  4. Grazing consumers
Answer and explanation

Decomposers digest dead organic matter and make its chemical constituents available for further cycling. Many bacteria and fungi carry out this role. Not every bacterium or fungus has the same mode of nutrition, so the role depends on the organism’s activity.

  1. Gills
  2. Operculum
  3. Nostrils
  4. Median fins
  5. Lateral line
Answer and explanation

E: Lateral line

The lateral-line system contains mechanosensory receptors that respond to nearby water movement and vibration. It helps a fish detect surrounding disturbances. Gills exchange gases, the operculum covers the gills, and nostrils are associated with smell.

  5. Moves rapidly

In suitable light, a photosynthetic Euglena uses light energy to make organic food from carbon dioxide. This is autotrophic nutrition. Some Euglena are mixotrophs and can also obtain organic nutrients from their environment.

Mucor is a fungus and does not contain chlorophyll. It obtains nutrients by external digestion and absorption. Its body consists of hyphae; sporangia produce asexual spores, and compatible hyphae can participate in sexual reproduction.

1985 · Question 8

Bryophytes differ from flowering plants because bryophytes

  1. Live in moist habitats
  2. Are small plants
  3. Reproduce both sexually and asexually
  4. Have small leaves
  5. Lack true vascular tissues
Answer and explanation

E: Lack true vascular tissues

Bryophytes lack the true xylem and phloem of vascular plants. Flowering plants possess these tissues. Moist habitats, small size and both modes of reproduction are not unique to bryophytes; some mosses have specialised conducting cells without true vascular tissue.

1985 · Question 10

  2. Rootlets
  3. Hyphae
  4. Rhizoids
  5. Thalli
Answer and explanation

D: Rhizoids

Rhizoids help anchor a moss to its substrate. They are not true roots and do not provide the same vascular transport system. Much water absorption in mosses occurs across the plant surface rather than through a root system.

1985 · Question 11

In a typical dorsiventral angiosperm leaf, where is the xylem positioned relative to the phloem in a vascular bundle?

  1. Beside the phloem at the same level
  2. Surrounded by the phloem
  3. Above the phloem, towards the upper leaf surface
  4. Around the phloem
  5. In a separate bundle from the phloem
Answer and explanation

C: Above the phloem, towards the upper leaf surface

The xylem is usually on the upper, or adaxial, side of a typical leaf vascular bundle, with phloem towards the lower side. Xylem delivers water and mineral ions; phloem transports sugars and other organic substances.

  2. Central pith
  3. A wide cortex
  4. A narrow cortex
  5. Pericyclic fibres
Answer and explanation

B: Central pith

A typical young dicot stem has vascular bundles arranged around central pith. A typical monocot root also has pith at its centre, surrounded by vascular tissues. Cortex width and the arrangement of rays or fibres do not provide the same shared defining comparison.

1985 · Question 16

  1. Anaemia
  2. Impaired skeletal growth
  3. Sterility
  4. Goitre
  5. Beriberi
Answer and explanation

B: Impaired skeletal growth

Calcium is a major mineral component of bone. Inadequate calcium can impair normal bone mineralisation and skeletal development. This effect differs from the classic deficiency associations of iron with anaemia, iodine with goitre and thiamine with beriberi.

  1. Chyme

  3. Glycogen
  4. Paste
  5. Roughage
Answer and explanation

A: Chyme

Chyme is the semifluid mixture formed as food is churned with gastric secretions. It passes through the pylorus into the duodenum in controlled amounts. Glycogen is a storage carbohydrate, and roughage refers to dietary fibre.

  1. Supply oxygen
  2. Filter lymph and help remove bacteria
  3. Form red blood cells
  4. Supply amino acids
  5. Supply simple sugars
Answer and explanation

Lymph passes through nodes containing macrophages and lymphocytes. These structures filter lymph and support immune responses to microbes and foreign material. Red blood-cell formation normally takes place in bone marrow, while oxygen is transported mainly by blood.

1985 · Question 19

Which large vein returns blood from the head and arms towards the right atrium of the heart?

  1. Aorta
  2. Inferior vena cava
  3. Superior vena cava
  4. Pulmonary vein
  5. Pulmonary artery
Answer and explanation

C: Superior vena cava

Veins from the head and upper limbs drain into the brachiocephalic veins and then the superior vena cava. This vessel enters the right atrium. The inferior vena cava returns blood chiefly from the lower body, and pulmonary veins return blood from the lungs.

1985 · Question 21

Considering ABO compatibility only for red-cell transfusion, which blood group can receive red cells from all four ABO groups?

  1. O
  2. A
  3. B
  4. AB
  5. AS
Answer and explanation

D: AB

Group AB has both A and B antigens and normally lacks anti-A and anti-B antibodies. In the simplified ABO red-cell model, it can receive red cells from O, A, B or AB. Real transfusions also require other compatibility checks; AS is a haemoglobin genotype, not an ABO group.

1985 · Question 22

Which statement about common forms of anaerobic glucose breakdown is INCORRECT?

  1. Muscle cells can produce lactate
  2. Some organisms produce carbon dioxide
  3. Lactic-acid bacteria can produce lactate
  4. No usable energy is obtained
  5. Some germinating seeds can produce ethanol
Answer and explanation

D: No usable energy is obtained

Glycolysis can provide a small net ATP yield without oxygen. Fermentation regenerates NAD⁺ so glycolysis can continue. Products depend on the organism: lactate is associated with lactic fermentation, while ethanol and carbon dioxide occur in alcoholic fermentation.

1985 · Question 23

Which combination describes a typical vertebrate gas-exchange surface? 1. Moist; 2. Well supplied with blood; 3. Selectively permeable; 4. Freely permeable to all substances; 5. Dry.

  1. 1, 2, 3
  2. 1, 2, 5
  3. 2, 3, 5
  4. 2, 4, 5
  5. 1, 3, 5
Answer and explanation

A: 1, 2, 3

A gas-exchange surface stays moist so gases can dissolve, and a blood supply maintains concentration gradients. Its thin cellular barrier allows respiratory gases to diffuse without being freely permeable to every substance. A dry barrier would not provide the usual efficient exchange conditions.

Region 2 is the cortex and region 4 is the medulla. Renal tubules in these regions reabsorb substances from tubular fluid into the body. Much nutrient and ion reabsorption occurs in the proximal tubules of the cortex, while other tubular segments contribute in the medulla.

  2. Skull and limbs

  5. Girdles and limbs
Answer and explanation

The appendicular skeleton includes the limb bones and the pectoral and pelvic girdles that connect them to the body. The skull belongs to the axial skeleton. Phalanges, ulna and radius are only subsets of the appendicular bones.

1985 · Question 30

A maize grain is botanically classified as a fruit because it

  1. Has a large endosperm
  2. Develops from an ovary
  3. Belongs to a monocot plant
  4. Has neither plumule nor radicle
  5. Shows hypogeal germination
Answer and explanation

B: Develops from an ovary

A maize grain is a caryopsis: a dry, one-seeded fruit whose fruit wall is closely fused with the seed coat. Its development from the ovary identifies it as a fruit. The embryo inside still contains structures that form the root and shoot.

1985 · Question 31

Protandry in a bisexual flower can

  1. Make the flower necessarily unisexual
  2. Leave its androecium undeveloped
  3. Remove its androecium
  4. Make insect pollination compulsory
  5. Reduce self-pollination within that flower
Answer and explanation

E: Reduce self-pollination within that flower

In protandry, pollen is released before the stigma of the same flower becomes receptive. The timing reduces the opportunity for self-pollination within that flower. It does not guarantee that pollen cannot move between different flowers on the same plant.

An Irish potato tuber is an enlarged underground stem. Its eyes are buds associated with nodes, and they can produce new shoots. These stem features distinguish it from a storage root.

  4. Anatomy

Phenotype includes observable characteristics such as form, physiology and behaviour. It arises through the interaction of inherited information and environmental influences. Genotype refers to genetic constitution rather than the full set of expressed traits.

  1. ADP
  2. CNS
  3. RNA
  4. ATP
  5. DNA
Answer and explanation

DNA stores the genetic information inherited by cellular organisms. RNA has important roles in expressing that information, but cellular genomes are DNA-based. Some viruses have RNA genomes; viruses are not cellular organisms.

  2. Epicotyl
  3. Epigeal
  4. Hypogeal
  5. Plumule
Answer and explanation

C: Epigeal

In epigeal germination, elongation lifts the cotyledonary region above the soil surface. In hypogeal germination the cotyledons remain below ground. Hypocotyl, epicotyl and plumule name parts of the developing shoot, not this germination category.

  5. Manufacture of blood cells alone
Answer and explanation

D: Chemical coordination through hormones

Endocrine glands release hormones that coordinate activity in target tissues. These signals influence many processes, including metabolism, growth and aspects of water balance. Nervous impulses are the main signalling mode of the nervous system.

1985 · Question 40

  1. Motor neurons
  2. Effector muscles
  3. Dendrites alone
  4. Sensory neurons
  5. Synapses alone
Answer and explanation

D: Sensory neurons

Sensory, or afferent, neurons carry information from receptors towards the central nervous system. Motor neurons carry commands towards effectors such as muscles. Dendrites and synapses participate in signalling but do not name the complete functional neuron type requested.

1985 · Question 43

  1. Fish and human
  2. Human and dog
  3. Snail and human
  4. Human and freshwater snail
  5. Fish and snail
Answer and explanation

D: Human and freshwater snail

Adult Schistosoma worms mature and reproduce sexually in the human definitive host. Larval development and multiplication occur in a suitable freshwater snail, the intermediate host. Infective cercariae leave the snail and can penetrate human skin.

  5. Dysentery

1985 · Question 46

Which is NOT a usual direct measure for protecting soil from erosion?

  1. Planting cover crops
  2. Contouring sloping ground
  3. Terracing slopes
  4. Laying mulch
  5. Burning off vegetation
Answer and explanation

E: Burning off vegetation

Cover crops and mulch protect the soil surface. Contouring and terracing help reduce the speed or concentration of runoff. Burning off vegetation removes protective cover and can expose the surface to greater erosion, rather than serving as a direct erosion-control measure.

1985 · Question 47

For otherwise comparable soils, which general composition favours high total water retention?

  1. Rich in sand, poor in humus and without clay
  2. Rich in clay and sand but poor in humus
  3. Rich in clay and humus but poor in sand
  4. Rich in clay but poor in humus and without sand
  5. Rich in sand and humus but poor in clay
Answer and explanation

C: Rich in clay and humus but poor in sand

Clay has small pores and a large surface area, while humus also helps hold water. A soil rich in both therefore tends to retain considerable water. Total retained water is not the same as water readily available to plants, because some water may be held too tightly.

1985 · Question 48

The mineral particles in soil originate mainly from

  1. Humus
  2. Water
  3. Microorganisms
  4. Weathered rock
  5. Organic matter
Answer and explanation

D: Weathered rock

Physical and chemical weathering break down rocks and alter minerals, producing the mineral fraction of soil. Humus comes from decomposed organic material. Water and organisms contribute to weathering but are not themselves the main mineral parent material.

1985 · Question 49

Which listed process returns nitrogen gas to the atmosphere?

  1. Denitrification by bacteria
  2. Breakdown of ammonium salts alone
  3. Nitrification by bacteria
  4. Nitrogen fixation in root nodules
  5. Egestion, death and decay alone
Answer and explanation

A: Denitrification by bacteria

Denitrifying microorganisms reduce oxidised nitrogen compounds through a pathway that can release nitrogen gas. Nitrogen fixation moves atmospheric nitrogen into biologically usable compounds, while nitrification converts ammonium to more oxidised forms such as nitrate.

1985 · Question 50

A dry soil sample receives 50 mL of water and 35 mL drains out. If evaporation and other losses are negligible, what percentage of the added water is retained?

  1. 46.7%
  2. 25.0%
  3. 20.0%
  4. 30.0%
  5. 58.3%
Answer and explanation

D: 30.0%

Retained water = 50 − 35 = 15 mL. As a percentage of the 50 mL added, this is (15 ÷ 50) × 100 = 30%. This calculation uses the water added as the denominator; soil mass or soil volume would be needed for a soil-water-content percentage.

The mouthparts of an adult housefly are adapted for

  1. Lapping and sponging
  2. Sucking and chewing
  3. Piercing and sucking
  4. Chewing and lapping
  5. Biting and chewing
Answer and explanation

A: Lapping and sponging

An adult housefly takes in liquid food using sponging mouthparts. Saliva can help dissolve suitable solid food before it is taken up. Its mouthparts do not chew solid pieces or pierce skin like those of a biting fly.

All three reproduce without seeds. Ferns have vascular tissues and distinct organs, whereas mosses and liverworts are bryophytes. Their habitats vary, and none produces flowers.

  2. Is green because its cells contain chloroplasts
  3. Is the dominant plant generation
  4. Bears the sexual organs
  5. Is attached to the ground by rhizoids
Answer and explanation

C: Is the dominant plant generation

The prothallus is the small gametophyte that bears the sex organs. The much larger leafy fern is the dominant sporophyte generation. A typical free-living prothallus is green and attached to its substrate by rhizoids.

1984 · Question 7

Which cell constituent is not normally present in animal cells but is present in photosynthetic plant cells?

  1. Mitochondria
  2. Chloroplasts
  3. Ribosomes
  4. Golgi apparatus
  5. Vacuoles
Answer and explanation

B: Chloroplasts

Chloroplasts carry out photosynthesis in green plant cells. Typical animal cells do not have chloroplasts. Mitochondria, ribosomes and Golgi apparatus occur in both groups, and animal cells can also contain small vacuolar compartments.

1984 · Question 10

  1. Amnion
  2. Chorion
  3. Umbilical cord
  4. Oviduct
  5. Placenta
Answer and explanation

E: Placenta

The placenta is the main exchange organ between maternal and fetal circulations. Oxygen and nutrients cross its exchange surface and reach the fetus through the umbilical circulation. Maternal and fetal blood normally remain in separate circulations.

1984 · Question 12

At equal pressure, osmosis between dilute solutions separated by a membrane permeable to water but not the solute is the net movement of

  1. Solute molecules from higher to lower concentration
  2. Solute molecules from lower to higher concentration
  3. Water from higher to lower solute concentration without a membrane
  4. Water across the membrane from lower to higher solute concentration
  5. Water across the membrane from higher to lower solute concentration
Answer and explanation

D: Water across the membrane from lower to higher solute concentration

Water moves from the more dilute solution, which has higher water potential, towards the more concentrated solution through the selectively permeable membrane. The direction stated assumes equal pressure and a solute that cannot cross the membrane.

1984 · Question 14

  1. Upward movement
  2. Controlling rolling movement
  3. Downward movement
  4. Steering
  5. Buoyancy
Answer and explanation

B: Controlling rolling movement

Dorsal and anal fins help stabilise the fish and resist unwanted rotation such as rolling. Their contribution varies with species and swimming behaviour. Other fins can assist steering, while buoyancy depends on body density and, in many bony fish, a swim bladder.

1984 · Question 16

Which blood component forms the initial plug at a damaged vessel and helps the clotting process?

  1. Leucocytes
  2. Platelets
  3. Haemolymph
  4. Haemoglobin
  5. Erythrocytes
Answer and explanation

B: Platelets

Platelets adhere at a damaged vessel and aggregate to form a temporary plug. They also support reactions that lead to a fibrin clot. Coagulation involves plasma factors as well as platelets; it is not caused simply by blood touching air.

1984 · Question 17

Pepsin is a digestive enzyme that breaks down

  1. Cellulose into glucose
  2. Carbohydrates into simple sugars
  3. Proteins into smaller peptides
  4. Fats into glycerol and fatty acids
  5. Sucrose into glucose and fructose
Answer and explanation

C: Proteins into smaller peptides

Pepsin begins protein digestion in the acidic stomach, cleaving proteins into smaller peptide fragments. It does not digest cellulose, fats or sucrose. Further protein digestion takes place in the small intestine.

AI-reviewed · source checked

1984 · Question 18

Alcoholic fermentation of glucose by yeast produces

  1. Carbon dioxide and ethanol
  2. Carbon dioxide and water
  3. Carbon dioxide and oxygen
  4. Carbon dioxide and glucose
  5. Ethanol and water
Answer and explanation

A: Carbon dioxide and ethanol

Yeast converts pyruvate into acetaldehyde, releasing carbon dioxide, and then reduces acetaldehyde to ethanol. This regenerates NAD⁺ so glycolysis can continue without oxygen. Carbon dioxide also helps bread dough rise.

1984 · Question 21

In the Biuret test, protein is mixed with sodium hydroxide solution. Which chemical is then added for a positive reaction?

  1. Mercurous nitrate
  2. Copper sulphate
  3. Mercuric nitrate
  4. Sodium carbonate
  5. Ammonium hydroxide
Answer and explanation

B: Copper sulphate

Copper(II) ions in alkaline solution interact with peptide bonds to produce a violet colour. Copper sulphate supplies these ions, while sodium hydroxide provides the alkaline conditions. The result indicates peptide bonds rather than a particular protein identity.

AI-reviewed · source checked

1984 · Question 22

Loss of pancreatic digestive secretions would impair digestion of which combination of food substances?

  1. Starch
  2. Starch, proteins and fats
  3. Oils and fats
  4. Proteins
  5. Carbohydrates and fats
Answer and explanation

B: Starch, proteins and fats

Pancreatic juice supplies amylase for starch, proteases for proteins and lipase for fats. Losing these secretions can therefore impair digestion of all three groups. Other digestive secretions may still act, so impaired digestion does not mean every digestive process stops.

1984 · Question 26

Which region normally has the same number of vertebrae in a rabbit, rat and human?

  1. Caudal
  2. Thoracic
  3. Lumbar
  4. Cervical
  5. Sacral
Answer and explanation

D: Cervical

Rabbits, rats and humans normally have seven cervical vertebrae. This is the neck region, including the atlas and axis. The numbers in the thoracic, lumbar, sacral and caudal regions differ between these animals.

1984 · Question 29

In the usual angiosperm model, how many nuclei are present in the mature pollen tube after generative-cell division but before discharge, while the tube nucleus remains intact?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 5
  4. 6
  5. 7
Answer and explanation

B: 3

The generative cell divides to form two sperm cells, each with a nucleus. Together with the vegetative tube nucleus, this gives three nuclei. The count refers to this defined stage, before the sperm are discharged for double fertilisation.

1984 · Question 31

Which part of a vertebrate brain is especially associated with coordination of muscular activity and balance?

  1. Olfactory lobes
  2. Cerebral hemispheres
  3. Optic lobes
  4. Cerebellum
  5. Spinal cord
Answer and explanation

D: Cerebellum

The cerebellum helps coordinate movement, timing and balance by comparing intended movement with sensory feedback. It works with other parts of the nervous system rather than acting alone. Well-coordinated movement is therefore especially associated with cerebellar function.

1984 · Question 32

Which adaptation is primarily associated with swimming rather than bird flight?

  1. Streamlined body shape
  2. Powerful flight muscles
  3. Reduced body mass
  4. A broad sternum for muscle attachment
  5. Webbed feet
Answer and explanation

E: Webbed feet

Webbed feet spread the force of a backward stroke over a larger area of water, aiding swimming. Streamlining, strong flight muscles, a suitable sternum and reduced mass can support flight. A swimming bird may also fly, but the webbing is chiefly a swimming adaptation.

1984 · Question 33

What spatial pattern is most directly recorded by a line transect in an ecological survey?

  1. The total number of plants and animals in the whole habitat
  2. The entire population of a plant species
  3. The distribution of organisms along a line
  4. The heights of all trees in a section of forest
  5. The total number of young plants across the forest
Answer and explanation

C: The distribution of organisms along a line

A line transect records organisms or vegetation encountered along a selected line. It can show how occurrence changes across a habitat or environmental gradient. Estimating the total population of a whole habitat requires an appropriate sampling design and further analysis.

1984 · Question 37

In the usual forward flow through a systemic vascular bed, blood does NOT flow from

  1. Arteries to arterioles
  2. Arterioles to capillaries
  3. Capillaries to venules
  4. Arterioles back into the supplying artery
  5. Venules to veins
Answer and explanation

D: Arterioles back into the supplying artery

Blood normally travels from arteries into smaller arterioles, through capillaries, then into venules and veins. Flow back from arterioles into their supplying artery reverses this usual sequence. Local pressure changes and pathological reverse flow are outside this normal-flow comparison.

1984 · Question 39

The axial skeleton of a mammal does not include the bones of the

  1. Skull
  2. Tail
  3. Limbs
  4. Back
  5. Neck
Answer and explanation

C: Limbs

The axial skeleton includes the skull and vertebral column, including cervical, trunk and caudal regions where present. Limb bones belong to the appendicular skeleton together with their supporting girdles. The limbs are therefore the exception.

1984 · Question 41

Green plants support food webs chiefly through their role as

  1. Primary consumers
  2. Producers
  3. Decomposers
  4. Secondary consumers
  5. Scavengers
Answer and explanation

B: Producers

Photosynthetic plants use light energy to make organic compounds from carbon dioxide. This primary production supplies food and stored chemical energy to many other organisms in a food web. Plants also respire, but that does not remove their producer role.

  1. Relative humidity
  2. Altitude
  3. Wind speed
  4. Turbidity
  5. Salinity
Answer and explanation

An anemometer measures wind speed. A wind vane instead indicates wind direction. Relative humidity, water turbidity and salinity require different instruments or methods.

1984 · Question 43

Which is a normal major component of clean air rather than an air pollutant at its usual atmospheric proportion?

  1. Noise
  2. Smoke
  3. Sulphur dioxide
  4. Carbon monoxide
  5. Molecular nitrogen
Answer and explanation

E: Molecular nitrogen

Molecular nitrogen makes up about 78% of dry air and is a normal atmospheric constituent. This differs from reactive nitrogen compounds that can contribute to pollution. Smoke, sulphur dioxide and carbon monoxide are pollutants, while excessive noise is environmental pollution.

1984 · Question 44

Which group consists entirely of abiotic environmental factors?

  1. Salinity, tide, plankton and turbidity
  2. Temperature, pH and soil insects
  3. Wind, altitude, humidity and light
  4. Conifers, wind, pH and rainfall
  5. Soil, water, bacteria and salinity
Answer and explanation

C: Wind, altitude, humidity and light

Abiotic factors are nonliving physical or chemical features of the environment. Wind, altitude, humidity and light all fit that description. Plankton, insects, conifers and bacteria are living organisms and are biotic components.

1984 · Question 45

Which disease, cause, symptom and transmission combination is correct?

  1. Cholera — virus — severe diarrhoea — contaminated water
  2. Malaria — protozoan — high fever — ordinary contact with an infected person
  3. Syphilis — virus — sexually transmitted disease — sexual contact
  4. Smallpox — virus — blister-like skin lesions — close contact with an infected person
  5. Sleeping sickness — bacterium — tiredness and disturbed sleep — tsetse-fly bite
Answer and explanation

D: Smallpox — virus — blister-like skin lesions — close contact with an infected person

Smallpox was caused by variola virus and could spread through close contact, producing characteristic skin lesions. Cholera and syphilis are bacterial diseases. Malaria and sleeping sickness are caused by protozoan parasites, with mosquito and tsetse-fly transmission respectively.

1984 · Question 50

Which sequence orders water-erosion forms from removal across a broad surface towards progressively larger channels?

  1. Rill → sheet → gully
  2. Gully → rill → sheet
  3. Sheet → gully → rill
  4. Sheet → rill → gully
  5. Rill → gully → sheet
Answer and explanation

D: Sheet → rill → gully

Sheet erosion removes soil across a relatively broad surface. Concentrated runoff can cut small rills, and larger channels are called gullies. This is a sequence of increasing channel development, not a rule that every site must pass through every stage.

1983 · Question 1

Root hairs develop as extensions of cells in the

  1. Root apex
  2. Epidermis of roots
  3. Vascular bundles
  4. Endodermis
  5. Pericycle
Answer and explanation

B: Epidermis of roots

A root hair is a tubular extension of a root epidermal cell. It increases the surface area available for absorbing water and mineral ions. The pericycle lies deeper inside the root and gives rise to lateral roots rather than root hairs.

1983 · Question 2

Enable JavaScript to verify this question's diagram before viewing its answer.

The diagram represents a cross-section of part of a dicot plant. Which feature can be used to identify the plant part shown?

  1. Position of 7
  2. Its circular nature
  3. Number of 5
  4. Presence of 3
  5. Width of 2
Answer and explanation

A: Position of 7

The central star-shaped xylem, with phloem between its arms, is characteristic of a young dicot root. Dicot stems instead usually have separate vascular bundles arranged in a ring. A circular outline alone does not distinguish a root from a stem.

1983 · Question 3

Enable JavaScript to verify this question's diagram before viewing its answer.

The diagram represents a cross-section of part of a dicot plant. The main function of 6 is to

  1. Separate 5 from 7
  2. Produce more of 5 and 7
  3. Produce cork
  4. Translocate water and mineral salts
  5. Conduct carbon dioxide to the other parts
Answer and explanation

B: Produce more of 5 and 7

The vascular cambium divides to produce secondary xylem towards the inside and secondary phloem towards the outside. It therefore adds more of the tissues labelled 7 and 5. Cork is produced by cork cambium, a different meristem.

1983 · Question 4

Enable JavaScript to verify this question's diagram before viewing its answer.

The diagram represents a cross-section of part of a dicot plant. The main function of 4 is to

  1. Surround the inner tissues
  2. Produce cork
  3. Produce root hairs
  4. Produce lateral roots
  5. Produce more of 3
Answer and explanation

D: Produce lateral roots

Lateral roots arise from the pericycle, the cell layer just inside the endodermis. New root primordia develop internally and grow out through the surrounding tissues. Root hairs instead arise from epidermal cells on the root surface.

1983 · Question 5

In a typical dicot leaf, guard cells differ from ordinary epidermal pavement cells because they

  1. Have no definite shape
  2. Lack nuclei
  3. Are defined by being smaller
  4. Contain chloroplasts
  5. Lack vacuoles
Answer and explanation

D: Contain chloroplasts

In a typical dicot leaf epidermis, guard cells contain chloroplasts whereas ordinary epidermal pavement cells usually do not. Guard cells also possess nuclei and vacuoles. Their changes in turgor regulate the opening of the stomatal pore.

1983 · Question 7

In botanical growth-form terminology, herbs differ from shrubs because herbs

  1. Do not produce fruits
  2. Are necessarily useful to herbalists
  3. Have nonwoody stems
  4. Are only annuals
  5. Are only perennials
Answer and explanation

C: Have nonwoody stems

In botanical usage, herbs are herbaceous plants with nonwoody above-ground stems. Shrubs retain woody stems. Herbs can be annual, biennial or perennial, and the word does not mean that the plant must be medicinal.

1983 · Question 9

A hydrogen-to-oxygen atomic ratio of 2:1 is commonly associated with which class of organic compounds?

  1. Proteins
  2. Carbohydrates
  3. Fats
  4. Fatty acids and glycerol
  5. Amino acids
Answer and explanation

B: Carbohydrates

Many common carbohydrates have hydrogen and oxygen atoms in a 2:1 ratio, as in glucose, C₆H₁₂O₆. This ratio is a useful introductory clue, but it does not alone prove that an unknown compound is a carbohydrate; there are exceptions and other compounds with the same ratio.

1983 · Question 10

Which pair is especially associated with chlorophyll formation, one element being its central metal atom and the other being required for its synthesis?

  1. Magnesium and iron
  2. Calcium and potassium
  3. Calcium and sulphur
  4. Potassium and sulphur
  5. Phosphorus and potassium.
Answer and explanation

A: Magnesium and iron

Magnesium is the central metal atom in chlorophyll. Iron is required for processes involved in chlorophyll synthesis, although it is not the central atom of the chlorophyll molecule. Deficiency of either can therefore impair normal green pigmentation.

1983 · Question 15

Movement of a plant part whose direction is independent of the direction of an external stimulus is called

  1. Taxis
  2. Tropism
  3. Haptotropic movement
  4. Nastic movement
  5. Phototropism
Answer and explanation

D: Nastic movement

A nastic movement is a plant-part response whose direction is independent of the direction from which the stimulus arrives. In a tropism, the direction of growth depends on the stimulus direction. Phototropism concerns light, and thigmotropism concerns directional touch.

  1. Medulla oblongata
  2. Olfactory lobe
  3. Cerebellum
  4. Cerebrum
  5. Frontal lobe
Answer and explanation

C: Cerebellum

The cerebellum integrates sensory feedback and helps coordinate posture, balance and precise movements. It compares intended movement with incoming information and contributes corrective signals. Other brain regions also participate, but the cerebellum is the best answer here.

  2. 4
  3. 2
  4. 3
  5. 5
Answer and explanation

An axillary or lateral bud can grow into a daughter bulb, allowing vegetative multiplication. The fleshy scales supply stored food, the basal stem bears these tissues and the roots absorb water and minerals. The labelled lateral bud is part 3.

1983 · Question 18

The principal food-storage tissue in an onion bulb is its

  1. Stem
  2. Lateral buds
  3. Cotyledons
  4. Dry outer scale leaves
  5. Fleshy leaf bases
Answer and explanation

E: Fleshy leaf bases

The onion bulb stores much of its food in thick, fleshy leaf bases or scales. These surround a short basal stem. The dry outer protective scales are not the principal succulent storage tissue.

1983 · Question 20

What type of fruit is formed from a single flower having several free carpels?

  1. multiple fruit
  2. Simple fruit
  3. Aggregate fruit
  4. Dehiscent fruit
  5. Indehiscent fruit.
Answer and explanation

C: Aggregate fruit

An aggregate fruit develops from several separate carpels of a single flower. Each carpel contributes a fruitlet. A multiple fruit instead develops from flowers grouped in an inflorescence, while a simple fruit comes from one ovary.

1983 · Question 22

Which of the following diseases is NOT caused by a virus?

  1. Rinderpest
  2. Maize rust
  3. Newcastle disease
  4. Swine fever
  5. Cassava mosaic disease.
Answer and explanation

B: Maize rust

Maize rust is caused by fungi, such as Puccinia sorghi in common rust. The other listed diseases are viral: rinderpest, Newcastle disease, swine fever and cassava mosaic disease. A fungal rust pathogen is biologically different from a virus.

1983 · Question 24

An organism with two identical alleles at a particular gene locus is called

  1. A heterozygote
  2. A hybrid
  3. An allelomorph
  4. A homozygote
  5. A diploid
Answer and explanation

D: A homozygote

An organism with two identical alleles at a particular gene locus is homozygous at that locus. For example, AA and aa are homozygous genotypes, whereas Aa is heterozygous. Diploid describes chromosome-set number rather than whether the two alleles match.

1983 · Question 26

Banana, plantain and pineapple can be grouped together because they

  1. produce small seeds
  2. are multiple fruits
  3. produce suckers
  4. have runners
  5. have bulbils.
Answer and explanation

C: produce suckers

Banana, plantain and pineapple can all produce suckers used for vegetative propagation. These shoots can grow into new plants. Pineapple is a multiple fruit, but banana and plantain are not, so fruit type does not unite all three.

1983 · Question 29

The permanent dental formula 2 × (I 3/3, C 1/1, PM 4/4, M 2/3) gives 42 teeth. Which animal has this formula?

  1. Rabbit
  2. Adult human
  3. Young child
  4. Dog
  5. Sheep
Answer and explanation

D: Dog

An adult dog has, on each side, three upper and three lower incisors, one canine in each jaw, four premolars in each jaw, two upper molars and three lower molars. Doubling the one-side total gives 2 × (3 + 3 + 1 + 1 + 4 + 4 + 2 + 3) = 42 teeth.

1983 · Question 30

Which statement about insectivorous plants is NOT true?

  1. They obtain some nutrients by trapping and digesting insects
  2. They attract insects only for pollination
  3. They can grow in soils poor in nitrogenous nutrients
  4. They supplement their nitrogen supply by digesting insects
  5. Examples include butterworts, sundews and pitcher plants
Answer and explanation

B: They attract insects only for pollination

Insectivorous plants attract some insects as prey, not solely as pollinators. Digestion of prey supplies nutrients such as nitrogen, helping these plants live in nutrient-poor habitats. They generally still use photosynthesis to make carbohydrates.

1983 · Question 32

Starting just below the skull, which order correctly lists the vertebrae through the lumbar region?

  1. Axis, atlas, remaining cervical, thoracic, lumbar
  2. Atlas, remaining cervical, axis, thoracic, lumbar
  3. Atlas, axis, thoracic, remaining cervical, lumbar
  4. Atlas, axis, remaining cervical, thoracic, lumbar
  5. Atlas, thoracic, remaining cervical, axis, lumbar
Answer and explanation

D: Atlas, axis, remaining cervical, thoracic, lumbar

The atlas is the first cervical vertebra, C 1, and the axis is the second, C 2. The remaining cervical vertebrae follow, then the thoracic vertebrae and then the lumbar vertebrae. Atlas and axis are therefore part of the cervical region, not separate regions.

1983 · Question 37

Positive phototropic bending of a coleoptile is brought about by

  1. Auxin moving away from the region that elongates more
  2. An even auxin distribution across both sides
  3. Auxin inhibiting growth on the side that elongates more
  4. Auxin accumulating on the shaded side and promoting greater elongation there
  5. The absence of auxin in the coleoptile
Answer and explanation

D: Auxin accumulating on the shaded side and promoting greater elongation there

Under one-sided illumination, auxin becomes more concentrated on the shaded side of a coleoptile. At growth-promoting concentrations, cells there elongate more than cells on the illuminated side. The unequal elongation bends the shoot towards the light.

1983 · Question 38

The swollen storage root of cassava is not a stem tuber because it

  1. Is enlarged with food reserves
  2. Has an aerial shoot elsewhere on the plant
  3. Has other roots associated with it
  4. Lacks axillary buds
  5. Has an outer covering over its stored food
Answer and explanation

D: Lacks axillary buds

Cassava’s swollen storage organ is a storage root. It lacks the axillary buds associated with nodes on a stem tuber. Storing food alone does not make an organ a stem: roots and modified stems can both act as food stores.

1983 · Question 39

The main function of the middle-ear ossicles—malleus, incus and stapes—is

  1. Transmission of vibrations
  2. Regulation of air pressure
  3. Support of the inner ear
  4. Maintenance of balance during movement
  5. Secretion of oil
Answer and explanation

A: Transmission of vibrations

The malleus, incus and stapes form a chain that transmits vibrations from the eardrum to the oval window of the inner ear. This transfer helps sound energy enter the fluid-filled inner ear. Balance is mainly sensed by the vestibular apparatus, not the ossicles.

1983 · Question 40

Which instrument is used to assess water clarity by measuring the depth at which it disappears from view?

  1. Thermometer
  2. Secchi disc
  3. Rain gauge
  4. Hygrometer
  5. Wind vane
Answer and explanation

B: Secchi disc

A Secchi disc is lowered into water to measure the depth at which it disappears from view, providing a measure of water transparency. Cloudier water usually reduces this depth. The reading is a clarity indicator, not a direct laboratory turbidity reading in NTU.

Which sequence represents a plausible food chain, with arrows from food to consumer? 1. Toad; 2. Mucuna plant; 3. Grasshopper; 4. Snake; 5. Hawk.

  2. 1 → 2 → 3 → 4 → 5
  3. 2 → 1 → 3 → 4 → 5
  4. 2 → 3 → 1 → 5 → 4
  5. 2 → 3 → 1 → 4 → 5
Answer and explanation

E: 2 → 3 → 1 → 4 → 5

A plausible chain begins with Mucuna, the producer, followed by a plant-eating grasshopper, an insect-eating toad, a snake and a hawk. Arrows run from food to consumer, showing the direction in which energy passes along the chain.

1983 · Question 43

In Rhizopus, the storage carbohydrate is

  1. Glucose
  2. Paramylon
  3. Glycogen
  4. Starch
  5. Oil
Answer and explanation

C: Glycogen

Rhizopus is a fungus, and glycogen is a fungal storage polysaccharide. It consists of branched chains of glucose units. Starch is the usual plant storage polysaccharide, while oil is a lipid rather than a carbohydrate.

1983 · Question 44

Which statement about the rate of transpiration is INCORRECT? It is

  1. Dependent on temperature
  2. Affected by changes in light intensity
  3. Unaffected by humidity
  4. Dependent on air movement
  5. Affected by availability of water
Answer and explanation

C: Unaffected by humidity

Humidity affects the water-vapour gradient between a leaf and the air. At otherwise comparable conditions, high humidity generally reduces evaporation from the leaf. Temperature, light, air movement and water supply can also influence transpiration.

1983 · Question 45

An external skeleton with jointed appendages is absent in the

  1. Cockroach
  2. Spider
  3. Millipede
  4. Snail
  5. Dragonfly
Answer and explanation

D: Snail

A snail is a mollusc and lacks the jointed appendages characteristic of arthropods. Its shell is an external protective structure, but it is not the articulated arthropod skeleton. Cockroaches, spiders, millipedes and dragonflies are arthropods.

1983 · Question 46

Which statement is incorrect for the typical complete permanent human dentition, including all wisdom teeth? It has

  1. More molars than incisors
  2. No specialised diastema between tooth classes
  3. The same number of teeth in the upper and lower jaws
  4. A total of thirty-two teeth
  5. A total of six molars
Answer and explanation

E: A total of six molars

The complete permanent dentition has three molars in each of four quadrants, giving twelve molars. There are eight incisors, four canines and eight premolars, for a total of thirty-two teeth. Each jaw contains sixteen teeth.

1983 · Question 47

When a Spirogyra cell is placed in a salt solution with lower water potential than its cell sap, it initially

  1. Remains unchanged
  2. Takes up water and bursts
  3. Absorbs a little water
  4. Loses water and its protoplast shrinks
  5. Becomes turgid
Answer and explanation

D: Loses water and its protoplast shrinks

Water leaves the cell by osmosis towards the lower water potential outside. The protoplast loses volume and can pull away from the cell wall, causing plasmolysis. The rigid wall does not simply shrink along with the cell contents.

1983 · Question 48

In humans, urea is produced mainly in the

  1. Liver
  2. Urinary bladder
  3. Spleen
  4. Kidneys
  5. Gall bladder
Answer and explanation

A: Liver

The liver converts nitrogen from amino-acid breakdown into urea through the urea cycle. Blood then carries urea to the kidneys for excretion in urine. The urinary bladder stores urine; it is not the main site of urea synthesis.

1983 · Question 50

After glucose is absorbed from the ileum, which sequence gives the correct order of the major venous vessels on its route towards the heart?

  1. Ileum → hepatic portal vein → hepatic artery → vena cava → heart
  2. Ileum → hepatic portal artery → hepatic artery → vena cava → heart
  3. Ileum → hepatic portal vein → vena cava → heart
  4. Ileum → hepatic vein → vena cava → heart
  5. Ileum → hepatic portal vein → hepatic vein → vena cava → heart
Answer and explanation

E: Ileum → hepatic portal vein → hepatic vein → vena cava → heart

Absorbed glucose reaches the liver through the hepatic portal vein. Blood passes through liver sinusoids, leaves through hepatic veins and enters the inferior vena cava on the way to the heart. The listed vessel sequence leaves the intervening liver tissue implicit.
